## 2026-08-15 — Story 10.68 (T-10.65-refuse-both-fanout — extend the both-supplied refusal to products.py and publications.py)

Closes residual 3 of Story 10.65 (entry below). 10.65 made an ambiguous `product_id` + `handle` pair an error on the six product-resolving tools in `tools/catalog_hygiene.py`, but seven other tools took the same pair and still applied the silent `product_id`-wins precedence — so the codebase carried **two different rules for one parameter pair**. Trello: https://trello.com/c/dNn3u8P2 (Story 10.68, Epic 10).

**Call sites re-derived against `main` first (Implementation step 1).** The card's premise held: 7 tools across 2 modules — `products.py`'s `get_product` / `get_product_description` / `get_product_full`, and `publications.py`'s `get_product_publications` / `publish_product_to_channels` / `unpublish_product_from_channels` / `set_product_publications`. Both operations-layer docstrings still documented the precedence explicitly (`operations/products.py`: "``product_id`` wins when both are given"; `operations/publications.py`: "``product_id`` wins when both are supplied"), confirming deliberate prior art rather than an oversight. Note the card's paths say `ops/` — the actual package is `shopify/operations/`.

**Scope question settled before writing code (Implementation step 2): the `handle`-channel work is OUT for `publications.py`, and this is not a deferral of the same hazard.** `tools/publications.py::_resolve_product_gid_and_meta` is the documented non-participant of the T-9.5-resolver-fanout consolidation, and its `product_id` path is numeric-ID-only — `to_gid("Product", product_id)` is a bare f-string, so a full GID passed there double-wraps into `gid://shopify/Product/gid://shopify/Product/123`. That is a *different* failure from the one 10.64/10.65 chased. What matters for this story is that publications **already has a working `handle` channel**: `read_product_publications` passes a handle to `productByHandle` unwrapped, so an all-digit handle such as `2024` is already addressable there. The escape hatch 10.64 had to *build* for `catalog_hygiene` therefore already exists here, and refusing the both-supplied pair leaves publications in exactly the state 10.65 left `catalog_hygiene`: the wrong-product write is avoidable everywhere, not impossible (see the new residual below). No resolver change was needed, and the survivor justification recorded in T-9.5-resolver-fanout difference #6 is untouched.

**Approach 1 (refuse at the operations chokepoints) — the breaking-change argument made again, not inherited (Implementation step 3).** Seven MCP tools change contract and **three of them are writes** (`publish_product_to_channels`, `unpublish_product_from_channels`, `set_product_publications`); the other four are reads. The card asserts "four of the seven are writes" while naming three — the count is three, and the round-2 review caught the card's miscount propagating into this entry and into `_identifiers.py`. The blast radius is unknowable for the same reason it was in 10.65: these are tools invoked by arbitrary LLM clients, and "no caller found in this repo" is not "no caller exists". The argument that makes it acceptable is the failure mode, and it has to be stated for *these* tools rather than borrowed. Under the old precedence, an ambiguous pair on `publish_product_to_channels` / `unpublish_product_from_channels` / `set_product_publications` resolved the `product_id` product and silently discarded the handle — a **wrong-product mutation of publication state**, with nothing in the response indicating the handle was ignored. After the change the same call is a loud structured error, immediately diagnosable, costing one failed call. Silent corruption traded for a visible refusal is the same trade 10.65 made, and it is worth more here because three of the affected tools mutate. Approach 2 (writes only) was rejected: it re-introduces the read/write asymmetry 10.65 considered and removed. Approach 3 (document the divergence) fails the story's own acceptance criteria, which require a per-tool regression test proving refusal.

**Implemented at two chokepoints, not in seven signatures.** `operations/products.py::_require_discriminator` already guarded neither-supplied for all three products reads and now also rejects both-supplied; `operations/publications.py::read_product_publications` had no equivalent guard (it inlined `if product_id:`) and now calls the same one. The seven tools inherit the rule — `publications.py`'s four funnel through `_resolve_product_gid_and_meta`, which every one of them already wrapped in `try/except`, so no per-tool error plumbing was added there.

**The shared-helper question, answered explicitly because the card asked for it on the record: promoted, not paralleled.** `_require_discriminator` was private to `operations/products.py`, so `operations/publications.py` could not reuse it. The rule now lives once in a new `shopify/_identifiers.py` (`BOTH_IDENTIFIERS_ERROR`, `is_supplied`, `both_identifiers_supplied`, `reject_both_identifiers`), imported by both operations modules **and** by `tools/_product_resolver.py`, so catalog_hygiene's refusal and these seven tools' refusal state the same claim from one constant rather than two hand-kept copies. It sits under `shopify/` rather than `tools/` because `shopify/` must never import `tools/` — pinned by `tests/architecture/test_layering.py`.

**What "one rule" actually required — the round-2 review's most useful correction.** The first cut shared only the error *string* and decided "supplied" by bare truthiness, while `catalog_hygiene._is_supplied` strips whitespace and treats `None` as absent. That is one rule in name only, and it was a live regression: `get_product(product_id="123", handle="   ")` was **refused as ambiguous** while the six catalog_hygiene tools resolved the same input by `product_id` — so a client that fills unused schema fields with blank strings, exactly the client behaviour this family of stories keeps designing around, would have started getting refusals on a perfectly unambiguous call. The predicate is now promoted alongside the constant: `shopify._identifiers.is_supplied` is the definition and `catalog_hygiene._is_supplied` is an alias of it, so the thirteen tools cannot drift on *what counts as supplied*. Pinned by parametrized `""` / `"   "` / `None` tests in both new suites. Note also that "literally the same sentence" was overreach and has been corrected wherever it appeared: `_resolve_product` appends a `_cap`-bounded `— got product_id=… and handle=…` tail, so the two share a constant and a predicate, not a byte-identical string.

**The refusal is vetted in the tool, not inherited from underneath it — the other round-2 correction.** Placing the guard only at the operations chokepoints looked sufficient and was not, because `tools/publications.py` resolves sales channels over the network *before* it reaches a product. Three consequences, all verified against the built tools rather than argued: an ambiguous call still cost a round-trip (and warmed the cross-call channels cache); a channel-argument error **masked** the identifier error entirely — `publish_product_to_channels(product_id="111", handle="other", confirm=True)` answered `"Error: provide channel_names or publication_ids"` and never mentioned the ambiguous pair; and the refusal, arriving as a `ValueError` through each tool's generic `except Exception`, was rendered with `SCOPE_HINT` appended, telling the caller *"the app likely needs reinstall on the store with read_publications / write_publications granted"* when the actual fix was dropping one argument. That last one directly undercut this story's own justification — a refusal is only "immediately diagnosable" if it says what is wrong. All four publications tools now call the shared `tools/_product_resolver.identifier_error` as their first statement, which also absorbed the four duplicated inline neither-supplied checks (the same consolidation `products.py` got) and returns a string instead of raising, keeping the refusal out of the scope-hint handler. The operations-layer guards stay exactly as they were: they are the non-MCP-caller chokepoint, not the tool-layer message path.

**Both halves of the mistake now arrive in the same shape.** Neither-supplied returns `"Provide either product_id or handle."` (historical, pinned); both-supplied returns `"Supply product_id or handle, not both."` — same voice, same punctuation, and notably *no* `Error:` prefix on one and not the other, which is how the first cut had it. Because the tool-layer message is a constant with no caller value in it, there is nothing to bound with `cap` there; the reflection bound remains where values are actually echoed, in `_resolve_product`'s tail.

**One consequence of that layering, worth knowing before editing the message.** `_resolve_product` appends a `_cap`-bounded `— got product_id=… and handle=…` tail, but `_scrub.cap` lives under `tools/` and is unreachable from `shopify/`. Rather than duplicate the cap or reflect unbounded caller-controlled text at the operations layer, **the operations-layer message deliberately echoes no values**; each tool renders it through its own error path, where the reflection bound already applies (`tools/publications.py` via its existing `cap(str(e))`, `tools/products.py` via the new `_identifier_error` helper, which applies `cap` even though today's message is value-free, so a future edit that starts echoing inherits the bound structurally). SEC-27 (Story 10.67, exception-reflection capping) is **not** folded in, per the card's scope guard.

**What did *not* change, and is pinned by tests.** Single-channel calls on all seven tools resolve exactly as before — `product_id` alone still takes the by-id query, `handle` alone still takes the by-handle query and is never wrapped into a Product GID. The neither-supplied behaviour is untouched in both directions: `operations/products.py` still raises `"provide either product_id or handle"`, `operations/publications.py` still returns `(None, [], False)` rather than raising, and all seven tools still return their original `"Provide either product_id or handle."` string. `reject_both_identifiers` deliberately implements **only** the both-supplied half of the rule for exactly this reason — unifying the two neither-supplied behaviours is a separate contract decision and was not smuggled in here.

**Test note.** Every regression test uses a fixture where the two identifiers name **different** products, because the behaviour being removed is "the wrong one was silently chosen" — a fixture pointing both parameters at one product cannot tell that apart from a plain not-found. For the three publications writes the response scripts are deliberately *complete*, long enough for the mutation to fire under the old precedence; a short script would have let those tests pass on the fake client's "unexpected extra execute()" exhaustion instead of on the refusal, which is the exact false-green the tests exist to prevent. `test_read_product_publications_product_id_wins_when_both_given`, which pinned the old precedence, was replaced rather than deleted.

**Residual this story does NOT close.**

1. **The single-slot ambiguity, now repo-wide in scope rather than module-scoped.** A caller who puts an all-digit handle into `product_id` **alone** still gets bare-digit → numeric-ID classification and reads (or, on the publications writes, mutates) whichever product carries that number. This is the same limitation 10.64 accepted and 10.65 restated, unchanged by this story — refusing the *ambiguous* pair says nothing about the *unambiguous-looking but wrong* single value. Closing it needs the evidence of real-caller risk that made approach 2 unselectable in 10.65, and nobody has it yet. Separately, `publications.py`'s `product_id` path still double-wraps a full Product GID (no passthrough); that is the T-9.5-resolver-fanout survivor's documented contract, out of scope here, and unchanged.

### Closed

| # | Item | How it closed |
|---|------|----------------|
| T-10.65-refuse-both-fanout | ~~Story 10.65 refused a both-supplied `product_id` + `handle` pair on the six `catalog_hygiene.py` tools, but seven others took the same pair and still applied a silent `product_id`-wins precedence — `products.py`'s `get_product` / `get_product_description` / `get_product_full` and the four `publications.py` tools, **three of the seven being writes** — so one parameter pair was governed by two different rules and the wrong-product mutation the refusal exists to stop stayed reachable on the publication writes~~ | The rule is promoted to a new `shopify/_identifiers.py` (`reject_both_identifiers` + `BOTH_IDENTIFIERS_ERROR`) and applied at **two operations-layer chokepoints**, not in seven tool signatures: `operations/products.py::_require_discriminator` (which already guarded neither-supplied for all three products reads) now also rejects both-supplied, and `operations/publications.py::read_product_publications` — which had no shared guard, inlining `if product_id:` — calls the same helper, so all four publications tools inherit it through the single `_resolve_product_gid_and_meta` funnel they already wrapped in `try/except`. `tools/_product_resolver.py` imports the same constant, so catalog_hygiene's refusal states the same claim from one constant rather than a hand-kept lookalike (not byte-identical: `_resolve_product` appends a `_cap`-bounded `— got …` tail). The helper lives under `shopify/` because `shopify/` must not import `tools/` (pinned by `test_layering.py`), which also means it cannot reach `_scrub.cap` — so the operations-layer message deliberately **echoes no caller values**, and each tool applies the reflection bound in its own error path Round 2 moved the *message* to the tool layer: all seven now call the shared `tools/_product_resolver.identifier_error` as their first statement, which absorbed all seven duplicated inline neither-supplied checks, renders both halves of the mistake in one shape (`"Provide either product_id or handle."` / `"Supply product_id or handle, not both."`, neither carrying an `Error:` prefix the other lacks), and keeps the refusal out of publications' generic handler — which had been appending a reinstall-the-app scope hint to a pure argument error, one round-trip late, maskable by a channel-argument error. The predicate was promoted too (`is_supplied`, with `catalog_hygiene._is_supplied` now an alias): sharing only the string left `("123", "   ")` refused here and resolved there, a live regression for any client that fills unused fields with blanks. Only the both-supplied half of the rule is shared: the two operations modules still answer neither-supplied differently (raise vs. `(None, [], False)`), deliberately untouched. **Breaking change on seven MCP tools, argued for these tools rather than inherited from 10.65** — see the paragraph above; the trade is silent wrong-product corruption for a loud structured error. The `handle`-channel work was settled as **out of scope before implementation**: publications already passes handles to `productByHandle` unwrapped, so an all-digit handle is already addressable there and no resolver change was needed. 20 new tests across four suites, each watched fail first — per tool, a refusal test whose fixture names two *different* products and which asserts the `product_id` twin's GID is never addressed; for the three publications writes, additionally that no `publishablePublish`/`publishableUnpublish` fires under a response script complete enough to have carried the mutation through; plus single-channel and neither-supplied regression guards on both channels. `test_read_product_publications_product_id_wins_when_both_given` was replaced rather than deleted. README gains the two missing rows for `get_product_description` / `get_product_full` and a break note under each affected table; Story 10.65's residual 3 and `_identifier_channel`'s "deliberately scoped, not repo-wide" docstring are corrected in place rather than left contradicting the new state. |

## 2026-08-15 — Story 10.65 (T-9.5-mutator-handle — thread the explicit handle channel through the catalog-hygiene mutators)

The unclosed half of T-9.5-numeric-handle. Story 10.64 (below) added `_resolve_product`'s keyword-only `handle` channel — on which bare digits always mean a handle — but wired it into `get_product_metafields` alone, because that tool already had the parameter. The five mutators that resolve a product still took `product_id` only, so for them the ambiguity was expressible nowhere and the **wrong-product write** — the severe outcome the item was raised for — stayed reachable exactly as before. Trello: https://trello.com/c/R4dmF5Ea (Story 10.65, Epic 10).

**Call sites re-derived against `main` before designing (Implementation step 1)**, since PR #134 moved them: `grep -rn "_resolve_product\b\|_resolve_product_gid\b" src/` confirmed the premise held — five mutators on `product_id` only, `get_product_metafields` the sole tool exposing `handle`.

**Why approach 1 (add the channel) and not approach 2 (reject bare numerics on writes) — the evidence rule, applied.** Implementation step 2 required establishing real-caller risk *before* approach 2 could be selected, and set the tie-break in advance: if it cannot be established, approach 2 is not selectable on evidence. It could not be. The **documented** blast radius was known and small (2 of 8 `catalog_hygiene` docstrings advertise handle acceptance, 1 disclaims it); the **undocumented** usage was not knowable — `tests/live/` calls none of the five, the README carries no invocation examples, and no saved MCP client config or repo tooling references them. These are MCP tools invoked by arbitrary LLM clients, so "no caller found in this repo" is not "no caller exists". Approach 1 therefore won by the pre-committed default rather than by a fresh judgement call at implementation time.

**Which guarantee this buys, stated plainly (Implementation step 3).** Approach 1 makes the wrong-product write **avoidable everywhere** — every mutator now has a channel on which an all-digit handle resolves correctly. It does **not** make it *impossible*: a caller who puts an all-digit handle into `product_id` **alone** still gets bare-digit → numeric-ID classification and writes to whichever product carries that ID. That is the same limitation 10.64 accepted, now with an escape hatch on all six tools instead of one. Only approach 2 (or approach 3's endpoint) closes *that* case, and it remains gated on usage evidence nobody has yet.

**One narrower case was closed outright, beyond approach 1's wording.** The both-supplied variant of the same trap — one identifier, both slots — is now **refused** on the five write tools rather than resolved by precedence (residual 1 below, closed). That was free: `handle` is new on all five, so no caller can be passing both today. What remains open is strictly the single-slot case, where the caller has told us only one thing and it is genuinely indistinguishable from a numeric ID.

**Two adjacent correctness gaps fixed in passing, because the new channel exposed them.**

1. **Blank-identifier echoes.** Making `product_id` optional meant every message that echoed it verbatim would render blank on a handle-only call: `update_product_vendor` / `update_product_type` / `update_product_options`' "no product found for ''", `update_product_options`' "is not on product ''", and `update_variant_image_binding`'s "No product found with id ." plus its five preview/confirm/log head lines **and its two "…GIDs not on product" rejections**. All now report whichever identifier the caller actually supplied, via a single `product_ref` now returned by `_identifier_channel` itself, already stringified and `_cap`-bounded, so all nine reflection points in `update_variant_image_binding` plus the four sibling messages in the other tools inherit the bound structurally rather than by each site remembering to apply it.
2. **The cascading-default hazard — and why keyword-only, not a `None` sentinel.** Giving `product_id` a default forces defaults onto every parameter after it, which drops them from the FastMCP-generated schema's `required` list. For `update_product_vendor` that was **destructive and severe**: `vendor=None` is the *deliberate* clear signal, so an omitted `vendor` silently wipes the field — and with `product_id` optional the tool would have had **no required parameter at all**, making `{"handle": "x", "confirm": true}` a schema-valid silent field-wipe. The same shape threatened `product_type` (where `""`, not `None`, is the clear value). The first cut of this story defaulted `product_type`/`option` to `None` and leaned on their validators' existing `None` rejection; the triple-threat review correctly identified that this (a) does not generalise to `vendor`, whose `None` is meaningful, and (b) silently drops schema-level requiredness for all of them. **Resolution: every parameter after `product_id` is now keyword-only**, so `vendor`, `product_type`, `category` and `option` stay *required* in the generated schema while `product_id` is free to be optional. `product_type` and `option` revert to their original non-optional annotations. The clear paths are untouched and pinned by tests — an explicit `vendor=None` still clears, an explicit `product_type=""` still clears; only *omission* is now rejected. The cost is that these five tools can no longer be called positionally from Python; MCP invocation is name-based, and no test or in-repo caller used positional form.

**Residuals this story does NOT close — named here rather than left implicit, since the review raised each and the answers are judgement calls a later reader should be able to re-open.**

1. ~~**The both-supplied trap.**~~ **CLOSED before merge.** The first cut implemented the card's specified `product_id`-wins precedence, which left one wrong-product write reachable *through the new parameter*: a client holding a single identifier and filling *both* slots with it (a plausible LLM-client behaviour) still resolved an all-digit handle as a numeric ID and still mutated whichever product carried that number. **All six** product-resolving tools now **refuse** a both-supplied call before any network round-trip, via `_identifier_channel`, which passes both values through so `_resolve_product`'s own "Supply product_id or handle, not both" rejection fires and each tool renders it through its existing structured-error path. No second copy of that error string was added. This departs from the card's approach-1 wording, deliberately: for the five mutators it costs nothing in backward compatibility because `handle` is new on all five, and refusing the ambiguous call is the only outcome that cannot corrupt the wrong product.

An intermediate revision kept `product_id`-wins on `get_product_metafields` alone, on the reasoning that a wrong *read* merely misleads where a wrong *write* corrupts. **That exception was dropped before merge** — one rule across all six tools is easier for a caller to hold than a read/write split, and the old precedence silently discarded the handle, so a caller naming one product by ID and another by handle got data for the first with no indication the second was ignored. See "the one deliberate breaking change" below. One subtlety is load-bearing and pinned by its own test: `_resolve_product` normalises a non-string `product_id` to `""` *before* its both-check, so the helper stringifies before delegating — passing an int through unchanged would slip past the rejection and resolve by `handle`, reintroducing the silent wrong-target write in the one case the guard exists for.
2. **The neither-supplied message does not mention `handle`.** All five surface `"product_id must be a non-empty string."`, while the sibling `get_product_metafields` says `"At least one of product_id or handle is required."` — inconsistent guidance for the same caller mistake. Not fixed here. The round-2 review rebutted the original justification by pointing out that a *tool-level* pre-check — which is exactly what `get_product_metafields` does — would improve the message without touching the resolver contract. That is true but does not resolve it: an omitted `product_id` and an explicit `product_id=""` are indistinguishable at this layer, so any pre-check fires for both, and the `""` case is pinned by a pre-existing **tool-level** test (`test_update_product_category_empty_product_id_errors_without_network`), not merely by the resolver's contract. `get_product_metafields` can do it because it has carried its own message since inception with nothing pinning the alternative. Changing the five means changing a pinned observable string, which is a deliberate contract decision rather than the wording tweak it looks like.
3. ~~**The both-supplied refusal stops at this module's boundary — `T-10.65-refuse-both-fanout`.**~~ **CLOSED by Story 10.68** (entry above). Seven other tools took the same two `product_id` / `handle` parameters and still applied a silent `product_id`-wins precedence: `products.py`'s `get_product`, `get_product_description`, `get_product_full` (via `ops/products.py`, whose docstring stated "``product_id`` wins when both are given") and the four `publications.py` tools (via `ops/publications.py`). The hazard used to justify break 2 above — a caller who names one product by ID and a *different* one by handle gets data for the first with no indication the second was ignored — **was still live on those seven** when this story shipped. Not fixed *here*: they live in two other modules with their own contracts and test suites, and widening a refusal across nine tools deserved its own card rather than being appended to this one. Flagged by the round-2 deep review, which correctly caught that an earlier revision of the README row and docstrings asserted the rule held "on every tool that resolves a product". **The module-scoped wording this residual introduced — in this entry and in `_identifier_channel`'s docstring — is now itself stale and has been corrected**: since 10.68 the rule *is* repo-wide, and reading this residual as still-open would understate the current guarantee.

4. **`update_variant_image_binding`'s "No product found with id `<ref>`" now says "id" when the ref is a handle.** Left as-is: the wording pre-dates this story, is pinned by a pre-existing test, and is already tracked as a watched behavioural string under T-9.6-error-msg-shift — a third shift of the same message to fix an imprecision is not worth the churn.

**Two deliberate breaking changes, not one.** An earlier revision of this entry claimed there was only one and described the keyword-only conversion as affecting "only positional Python calls that MCP never makes". The round-2 review disproved that, and the correction matters because both breaks are visible to an MCP client:

**Break 1 — `update_product_vendor` now requires `vendor`.** Verified against the installed FastMCP: the generated schema moves from `required: []` to `required: ['vendor']`. Omitting `vendor` was previously schema-valid and *cleared the field* (`_normalize_vendor(None)` → clear), which is exactly the silent field-wipe the deep review flagged as High and the reason for the keyword-only conversion. So this break is the fix, not a side effect — but it is a break: a client that relied on bare `{"product_id": "..."}` to clear a vendor now gets a validation error. The same applies in principle to `product_type`, `category`, `option` and `variant_media`, though for those an omitted argument was already an error rather than a destructive default.

**Break 2 — `get_product_metafields` no longer applies `product_id`-wins.** Everything else in this story is additive: `handle` is new on the five mutators, so no caller can be passing both there. This one is different. `get_product_metafields` has carried *both* parameters since before 10.64, and 10.64 explicitly documented and pinned the precedence — so a caller supplying both today gets a structured error where it previously got data. Recorded as a genuine break rather than filed under the additive changes.

Justification: the precedence silently discarded the handle. A caller who named one product by ID and a *different* one by handle received data for the first with nothing indicating the second was ignored — a wrong-data-returned outcome of the same family as the wrong-product write, differing only in severity. Refusing surfaces the caller bug loudly at the cost of one failed call. The blast radius is unknown for the same reason approach 2 was unselectable — these are MCP tools invoked by arbitrary LLM clients — but the failure mode here is a loud, structured, immediately-diagnosable error rather than silent corruption, which is what makes the break acceptable where approach 2's was not. If it proves disruptive, restoring the precedence is a one-line change plus a test.

**Deliberately out of scope.** `update_product_pricing` (Story 9.3's open item — its `product_id` still takes numeric/GID only, no handle) is untouched; the sibling Unicode-digit work in `tools/_resolvers.py::_classify_no_fetch` and `_resolve_owner_gid_for_metafield` is Story 10.66. Neither was folded in, per the card's scope guard.

### Closed

| # | Item | How it closed |
|---|------|----------------|
| T-9.5-mutator-handle | ~~The `handle` channel added by Story 10.64 was exposed by `get_product_metafields` alone; the five catalog-hygiene mutators that resolve a product (`update_product_category`, `update_product_vendor`, `update_product_type`, `update_product_options`, `update_variant_image_binding`) took `product_id` only, leaving the wrong-product **write** reachable with no alternative channel available to the caller~~ | All five gain an optional `handle` parameter mirroring `get_product_metafields`, and **refuse a call that supplies both** rather than applying a precedence — closing the last wrong-product write reachable through the new parameter (see residual 1, closed). `get_product_metafields` adopts the same refusal, superseding the `product_id`-wins precedence Story 10.64 documented for it: the story's one genuine breaking change, justified above. `product_id` becomes optional on each (default `""`); **every parameter after it is keyword-only**, so `vendor`, `product_type`, `category`, `option` and `variant_media` stay in the generated MCP schema's `required` list instead of inheriting a cascading default. (An abandoned first cut instead defaulted `product_type`/`option` to the value their validator already rejects; that does not generalise to `vendor`, whose `None` is the deliberate clear signal, and it silently dropped schema requiredness for all of them.) New shared helper `catalog_hygiene._identifier_channel(product_id, handle) -> (pid_arg, handle_arg)` encapsulates the channel-splitting and the both-supplied refusal in one place across **all six** call sites — the five mutators plus `get_product_metafields`, which was the precedent the helper was modelled on and which now uses it instead of keeping a sixth hand-rolled copy — and makes the resolver's both-supplied `ValueError` **deliberately reachable** from every one of those surfaces: the helper passes both values through untouched precisely so that rejection fires, which is the refusal mechanism rather than an accident. (An earlier revision of this row claimed the opposite, describing the superseded `product_id`-wins design.) The helper treats `None` and blank strings as *absent* but any other non-string as *supplied*: routing a malformed `product_id` to its own channel yields a structured rejection, whereas treating it as absent would silently invert the precedence and write to the `handle`'s product instead — **no resolver change was needed**, per the card's step 5, and `_resolve_product` / `_resolve_product_gid` are untouched. Every message that echoed `product_id` verbatim now names the identifier actually queried (see the paragraph above). 18 new tests in `test_catalog_hygiene.py`, each watched fail against the pre-fix tree: per mutator, an all-digit-handle **write** test whose fixture puts the numeric handle (`"2024"`) and the numeric ID on two *different* products and asserts `gid://shopify/Product/2024` appears in no call at all, plus a precedence test — since replaced by a refusal test — and a not-found-names-the-handle test; cross-cutting, a Product-GID-via-`handle=` zero-round-trip guard (AC #4 — PR #134's passthrough still intact) and a neither-identifier-supplied no-network rejection. A further 12 tests landed with the review fixes: parametrized proof that each keyword-only required argument cannot be omitted, that an explicit `vendor=None` / `product_type=""` still clears, that a non-string `product_id` never hands the write to `handle` while `None` and whitespace still defer to it, that both previously-missed "…GIDs not on product" rejections name the handle, and that `product_ref` is `_cap`-bounded in reflected output. A further ~10 landed with the both-supplied refusal: parametrized proof that each of the five mutators refuses a both-supplied call before any network round-trip and never addresses the numeric-ID twin of the handle, that the refusal renders as a structured error rather than raising, that a blank `handle` alongside `product_id` is not ambiguous, that handle-alone still works, and that `get_product_metafields` refuses identically while both of its single-channel paths still resolve. Round-2 review added schema-level pins: the generated `required` list for each of the five tools, `product_id`/`handle` confirmed absent from it, and a guard that no product-resolving mutator presents an empty `required` list — the artefact the keyword-only safety property actually depends on, which a Python-level `TypeError` assertion does not prove. README tool table updated for all five rows; the superseded half of 10.64's "Accepted residual" paragraph corrected in place rather than left contradicting this state; `_resolve_taxonomy_category`'s stale ordering comment corrected, since it claimed a cheap-bail guarantee `update_product_category` does not actually provide. |

## 2026-08-14 — Story 10.64 (T-9.5-numeric-handle — resolve the numeric-handle ambiguity in the shared product resolver)

Residual of T-9.5-resolver-fanout (Story 10.62, below), raised by the code-quality reviewer during that story's triple-threat review and correctly deferred: 10.62 was a pure refactor with a zero-behavior-change scope guard, and this needed a behavior change. Trello: https://trello.com/c/P35Vcbl5 (Story 10.64, Epic 10).

**The blocker, answered first (Implementation step 1).** *Does Shopify actually permit a purely-numeric product handle?* **Yes — or at minimum, nothing prevents one.** `Product.handle` is documented as "A unique, human-readable string of the product's title. A handle can contain letters, hyphens (`-`), and numbers, but no spaces" (https://shopify.dev/docs/api/admin-graphql/latest/objects/Product); handles are auto-generated by lowercasing the title and collapsing whitespace/special characters to hyphens, so a product titled `2024` has nothing to strip and yields the handle `2024`. `productByHandle` documents no special handling for numeric input and no handle-vs-ID disambiguation mechanism. Merchants can also set a handle manually. **Evidence caveat, recorded deliberately:** this is *absence of prohibition* plus *digits explicitly permitted*, not a positive confirmation from a live store — no documented rule, community report, or API note excludes digits-only handles, and no documented live example was found either. That is decisive enough to rule out the "closed-safe, add a comment and a regression test" branch, because the resolver cannot rely on Shopify to prevent the input. Whether *this* store's catalog already contains an all-digit handle was **not** verifiable — the `get_products` connector tool was disabled throughout — but that only sets urgency, not whether to fix.

**Two defects, one line apart.**

1. **Numeric-handle misclassification.** `_resolve_product` classified `product_id` as numeric-ID-vs-handle on `stripped.isdigit()` with no tie-breaker, so an all-digit handle never reached `productByHandle` — it was wrapped into `gid://shopify/Product/<digits>`. Two silent outcomes: a "no product found" error naming the wrong lookup, or — when a different product happens to carry that numeric ID — **a read or write against the wrong product**. Every catalog-hygiene mutator routes through this resolver.
2. **`str.isdigit()` is Unicode-aware.** Superscript (`'²'`), fullwidth (`'２０２４'`) and Arabic-Indic (`'٤٢'`) digits all returned True and were wrapped into malformed GIDs like `gid://shopify/Product/²`. Shopify IDs are ASCII; these are neither a valid ID nor a valid handle.

**Approach chosen: explicit disambiguation on the tool surface (approach 3 of three groomed options).** The caller inventory (Implementation step 3, `grep -rn "_resolve_product\b\|_resolve_product_gid\b" src/`) found 7 live call sites, all in `catalog_hygiene.py`, and established that **separate `product_id` / `handle` parameters are already this codebase's house convention** — `products.py`'s `get_product` / `get_product_description` / `get_product_full`, four tools in `publications.py`, and `catalog_hygiene.py`'s own `get_product_metafields` all take both. The `catalog_hygiene` mutators that overload a single `product_id` are the outliers. Rejected alternatives, recorded so they are not re-litigated: *numeric-ID-first with handle fallback* fixes only the not-found outcome and leaves the wrong-product write untouched, since the fallback never fires when the ID hits; *rejecting bare numerics as ambiguous* (mirroring `_resolve_owner_gid_for_metafield`) is the only option that makes the wrong-product write impossible, but is a breaking change to every tool accepting a bare numeric today — the inventory showed a smaller blast radius than assumed (only 2 of 8 `catalog_hygiene` docstrings advertise handle acceptance, and one explicitly disclaims it), so it remains the option to revisit if the residual below ever becomes unacceptable.

**Accepted residual — ~~recorded, not silently left open, and narrower than it first reads~~ SUPERSEDED 2026-08-15 by Story 10.65 (T-9.5-mutator-handle, below); kept for the reasoning, corrected here rather than left contradicting the current state.** Approach 3 made the ambiguity expressible and avoidable **only where a tool actually exposes the `handle` channel**, which at the close of this story was `get_product_metafields` alone. The claim that the five mutators (`update_product_category`, `update_product_vendor`, `update_product_type`, `update_product_options`, `update_variant_image_binding`) "still take `product_id` only, so for them there is *no* alternative channel" **is no longer true** — Story 10.65 threaded `handle` through all five, so the caller always has a correct channel and the wrong-product write is now avoidable everywhere. What remains true, and is the residual that survives 10.65: bare digits in `product_id` still mean "numeric ID" on every tool, so a caller who puts an all-digit handle in `product_id` still gets the wrong product. That is the deliberate cost of backward compatibility and of preserving the zero-network-call path, since a handle-first lookup would add a round-trip to *every* numeric call. Making the wrong-product write **impossible** rather than merely avoidable still requires rejecting bare numerics on write tools — see 10.65's entry for why that was not selectable on evidence. The trade-off is documented at the `isdigit()` branch, in `_resolve_product`'s docstring, and in every affected tool's parameter docs.

**Contract movement, stated rather than glossed.** Two things did change for `_resolve_product_gid`'s three otherwise-untouched callers, and the earlier claim that they were "otherwise unchanged" was too strong:

1. **Non-ASCII-digit input now reaches the network.** `'²'` / `'٤٢'` / fullwidth digits previously satisfied the Unicode-aware `isdigit()` and returned `(gid, None)` with zero calls; they now fall through to `productByHandle`, so `update_product_category`, `update_variant_image_binding` and `_resolve_owner_gid_for_metafield` can reach one round-trip — and therefore the not-found and `Handle lookup failed (…)` strings — on input that never touched the network before. The GID it used to build was malformed, so this is a fix, not a loss; it is recorded because it is observable.
2. **The not-found message now interpolates whichever identifier was supplied**, not `product_id` unconditionally — superseding the description in difference #4 of the T-9.5-resolver-fanout entry below.

**Knock-on to T-9.5-resolver-fanout's difference #6.** That entry justified leaving `publications.py::_resolve_product_gid_and_meta` out of the consolidation *primarily* because it "takes `product_id` and `handle` as two separate tool parameters rather than one dispatched string — a different input contract". After this story `_resolve_product` has exactly that shape, so only the second half of the justification (its `product_id` path is numeric-ID-only, with no GID passthrough or handle detection) still stands. The survivor's status is not re-opened here — that would be a scope widening — but the stale half of its rationale is flagged so the next reader does not rely on it.

**Same defect class left standing in two sibling resolvers — deliberately out of scope, filed as follow-up.** `tools/_resolvers.py::_classify_no_fetch` still uses a bare Unicode-aware `stripped.isdigit()` before `to_gid("ProductVariant", …)`, so `'٤٢'` becomes a malformed `gid://shopify/ProductVariant/٤٢` — reachable from user-supplied `variant_media[].variantId` and the pricing tool's variant ids. `catalog_hygiene.py:676`'s `_resolve_owner_gid_for_metafield` likewise uses a raw `.isdigit()`, so its "ambiguous ownerId" rejection and this resolver's handle fallback now disagree on what counts as numeric. Both are the same bug class in different modules with their own callers and contracts; fixing them here would widen a story whose scope guard is explicit.

### Closed

| # | Item | How it closed |
|---|------|----------------|
| T-9.5-numeric-handle | ~~`_resolve_product` classified `product_id` as numeric-ID-vs-handle purely via `stripped.isdigit()`, so an all-digit Shopify handle was misread as a legacy product ID and silently resolved to the wrong product (or none); `str.isdigit()` additionally accepted non-ASCII digits and produced malformed GIDs~~ | `_resolve_product` gains a keyword-only `handle` parameter — the unambiguous channel on which bare digits **always** mean a handle. Crucially the channel skips *numeric* classification only: a Product GID passed via `handle=` still short-circuits with zero network calls and a wrong-type GID still fails fast with the same message, because a GID is never a valid handle and there is no ambiguity to protect against. (An earlier revision of this fix routed the whole `handle` channel straight to `productByHandle`; the deep review caught that this silently broke `get_product_metafields(handle=<GID>)` — previously zero-call and working, since the old `pid or hdl` collapse ran the full dispatch — into a wasted round-trip and a "no product found" error. Regression tests now pin GID passthrough on both channels.) A non-string `handle` raises rather than being silently discarded. `product_id` becomes optional and keeps its exact historical semantics (bare digits → numeric ID, GID passthrough, otherwise handle), so every existing caller is untouched. Supplying both raises `ValueError` before any network call rather than applying a precedence rule. The duplicated handle-lookup body is extracted to `_lookup_by_handle`. The numeric test is tightened to `stripped.isascii() and stripped.isdigit()`; non-ASCII digits now fall through to the handle lookup and simply do not resolve — deliberately **not** a new exception type, so `_resolve_product_gid`'s adapter contract is untouched. `catalog_hygiene._resolve_product_gid` gains a matching keyword-only `handle` passthrough and reports whichever identifier the caller supplied in its not-found message; its historical `(gid, error_str)` shape and its five error strings are preserved verbatim, and its four callers are unchanged **apart from the two observable movements recorded above** (non-ASCII digits can now reach the network; the not-found message names whichever identifier was supplied). **Confirmed live bug fixed:** `get_product_metafields` documented `handle` as "Resolved via productByHandle before the metafield query" but collapsed both parameters into `_resolve_product_gid(client, pid or hdl)` — an all-digit handle supplied through that tool's own documented, unambiguous parameter was classified as a numeric ID and read the wrong product, i.e. a caller doing everything right got silently bad data. It now routes `handle` through the keyword channel, preserving the documented `product_id`-wins precedence. **(That precedence was superseded 2026-08-15 by Story 10.65, which refuses a both-supplied call on all six product-resolving tools — see its entry above.)** 13 new tests (`test_product_resolver.py`: handle-channel dispatch with and without a snapshot query, stripping, not-found, both-supplied rejection, backward-compat numeric short-circuit, historical empty-input message, parametrized non-ASCII digit cases, ASCII regression; `test_catalog_hygiene.py`: the all-digit-handle regression, the numeric-`product_id` zero-lookup guard, and `product_id`-wins precedence) — each watched fail against the pre-fix tree before implementation. |

## 2026-08-14 — Story 10.62 (T-9.5-resolver-fanout — consolidate the six near-twin product-id resolvers, for real this time)

Story 10.11 (2026-05-27, see below) closed T-9.5-resolver-fanout once already, collapsing three of the four twins behind a shared `_resolve_product_with_queries` dispatch and leaving `_resolve_product_gid` alone as a deliberately different return shape. Nobody owned the item after that: Epic 10 shipped two more catalog-hygiene tools that each needed a product read and each copied the pattern instead of extending the shared helper, so the count crept 4 → 6 with the ledger still recording 4. **Re-verified before designing against it** (Implementation step 1): `grep -rn "def _resolve_product" src/` on 2026-08-13 returned six hits, confirming the card's premise — the stated trigger ("any Epic-10+ catalog-hygiene tool that needs a product read") had already fired, twice, silently. Trello: https://trello.com/c/0d7jgoMK (Story 10.62, Epic 10).

Behavioral differences found while diffing the six implementations (Implementation steps 2–3), and the decision made about each — **this is a pure refactor, so every one of these is preserved exactly, not unified**, even where preserving it meant carrying a small inconsistency forward on purpose:

1. **Two return shapes.** `_resolve_product_gid` (9.1) returned `(gid, error_str)`; the other three (`_resolve_product_with_queries` + its two thin wrappers, 9.2/9.4/9.5) returned `(gid, product_snapshot)`. *Decision:* keep both shapes at the call-site boundary. The new shared core standardizes on the snapshot-returning convention; `_resolve_product_gid` survives as a thin adapter reshaping the core's output back into its historical tuple, so none of its four callers change.
2. **Error signaling.** `_resolve_product_gid` returned `(None, error_string)` for bad input, never raising. `_resolve_product_with_queries` raised `ValueError` for the same bad input, relying on each of its three callers' own `try/except Exception`. *Decision:* the shared core raises (matches the majority convention); the adapter catches `ValueError` to preserve `_resolve_product_gid`'s non-raising contract.
3. **A one-character message drift.** `_resolve_product_gid`'s empty-input message carried a trailing period ("...non-empty string."); `_resolve_product_with_queries`'s raised message did not. No test asserts exact string equality (both are checked via substring), but the scope guard forbids any observable-behavior change, however small — *decision:* the adapter special-cases this one message to reproduce the trailing period byte-for-byte rather than silently dropping it.
4. **Not-found handling.** `_resolve_product_gid`'s handle-not-found path returned a specific message ("No product found with handle '...'."). The snapshot-returning three returned `(None, {})` silently and let the caller synthesize its own generic message ("Error: no product found for '...'."). *Decision:* the shared core always returns `(None, {})` on not-found (not an error at that layer); the adapter re-synthesizes `_resolve_product_gid`'s historical specific wording from the not-found result.
5. **Transport-failure handling.** `_resolve_product_gid` caught exceptions from its handle-lookup network call and returned them as an error string ("Handle lookup failed (...): ..."). The snapshot-returning three let transport exceptions propagate to the caller's own wrapper ("Error resolving product_id (...): ..."). *Decision:* the shared core does not catch (matches the snapshot convention); the adapter re-adds its own catch to preserve `_resolve_product_gid`'s wording.
6. **`tools/publications.py`'s `_resolve_product_gid_and_meta` is not a copy of this dispatch and was left out, as a justified survivor** (AC #1's "or the survivors are individually justified" clause): it takes `product_id` and `handle` as two *separate* tool parameters rather than one dispatched string, and its `product_id` path is numeric-ID-only — it calls `to_gid("Product", product_id)` directly with no GID-passthrough or handle detection, so a full GID or handle string passed as `product_id` would double-wrap into a broken GID (confirmed: every `test_publications.py` call site passes a bare numeric string, none exercise GID passthrough). Folding it into the shared dispatch would change publications.py's public input contract, which is out of scope for a pure refactor.

### Closed

| # | Item | How it closed |
|---|------|----------------|
| T-9.5-resolver-fanout | ~~Six near-twin `_resolve_product*` helpers: `_resolve_product_gid`, `_resolve_product_with_queries`, `_resolve_product_id`, `_resolve_product_id_for_type`, `_resolve_product_id_for_options` in `tools/catalog_hygiene.py`, plus `_resolve_product_gid_and_meta` in `tools/publications.py`~~ | New module `tools/_product_resolver.py::_resolve_product(client, product_id, *, query_by_id=None, query_by_handle=None)` is now the single place the numeric/GID/handle dispatch lives — placed under `tools/` (not inside `catalog_hygiene.py`) specifically so `publications.py` or any future tool module can import it without a layering violation. `_resolve_product_id`, `_resolve_product_id_for_type`, `_resolve_product_id_for_options`, and `_resolve_product_with_queries` are deleted outright — their three call sites (`update_product_vendor`, `update_product_type`, `update_product_options`) now call `_resolve_product` directly with their own query pair, migrated and suite-run one at a time per Implementation step 5. `_resolve_product_gid` survives as a thin adapter over the shared core (see the six behavioral differences above); its four call sites (`update_product_category`, `update_variant_image_binding`, `get_product_metafields`, the metafields owner-type resolver) are unchanged. `_resolve_product_gid_and_meta` survives as a documented, justified non-participant (difference #6 above). `grep -rn "def _resolve_product" src/` now returns exactly three hits — the one consolidated resolver plus its two individually-justified survivors — down from six. Also removed the now-dead `_PRODUCT_GID_PREFIX` module constant in `catalog_hygiene.py` (only the deleted functions referenced it). Direct tests for `_resolve_product` added in `tests/unit/tools/test_product_resolver.py` (14 tests: numeric/GID/handle × with/without query, not-found for both paths, all three validation-error cases, input stripping) — the four preserved-behavior differences in `_resolve_product_gid` remain covered by the pre-existing `test_catalog_hygiene.py` assertions, unmodified. `tests/architecture/test_layering.py` passes unmodified. CI clean in a fresh Python 3.11 venv built from `requirements-dev.lock`: `shopify-mcp-check-deps`, `ruff check .`, `ruff format --check .`, `mypy` (64 files), 1383 offline tests at 100% coverage, both `pip-audit --strict` runs clean. |

## 2026-08-13 — Story 10.61 (SEC-26 — pin CI marketplace actions to commit SHAs)

Supply-chain hardening. Raised as a **Low (security)** finding by Story 10.40's triple-threat review (SEC-13/SEC-14) and explicitly deferred there: "actions/checkout@v4 / actions/setup-python@v5 pinned to mutable tags rather than commit SHAs... pinning every Marketplace action to a SHA repo-wide is a separate tech-debt item — out of scope for SEC-13/SEC-14." This is that item. Trello: https://trello.com/c/ha8UdzQR (Story 10.61, Epic 10).

### Closed

| # | Item | How it closed |
|---|------|----------------|
| SEC-26 | ~~All six `uses:` steps across the three `.github/workflows/test.yml` jobs (`offline-tests`, `lint`, `dependency-audit`) referenced `actions/checkout@v4` and `actions/setup-python@v5` — mutable tags whoever controls the upstream repo can repoint at any commit, executed in a job with repository read access on every push/PR against `main`.~~ | Resolved each tag to the commit SHA it currently points at via `gh api repos/<owner>/<repo>/git/ref/tags/<tag>`, then cross-checked against `gh api repos/<owner>/<repo>/tags` to confirm the SHA matches the named release: `actions/checkout@v4` → `11d5960a326750d5838078e36cf38b85af677262` (`v4.4.0`), `actions/setup-python@v5` → `a26af69be951a213d495a4c3e4e4022e16d87065` (`v5.6.0`). All six lines now read `<owner>/<repo>@<40-char-sha> # <tag>`. Added `.github/dependabot.yml` with a `github-actions` ecosystem entry (weekly schedule) so the pins are bumped by PR instead of aging by hand — the same failure mode SEC-23 (Story 10.57) hit with an unmonitored transitive dependency pin. No `.github/dependabot.yml` existed before this story, so it was authored fresh rather than extended. `tests/architecture/test_ci_workflow_pins.py` (new) is the executable spec: asserts every `uses:` line across `.github/workflows/*.yml` is a 40-char commit SHA carrying a trailing version comment, so a future PR that reintroduces a mutable tag fails the offline suite instead of drifting silently. Verified on a pushed branch that all three jobs (`offline-tests`, `lint`, `dependency-audit`) still run and pass against the pinned actions — nothing local exercises the workflow itself, so that push was the only real verification of the pins working. |

## 2026-08-11 — Story 10.56 (SEC-22 — surface a startup warning when the webhook allowlist opt-out is active)

Operational hardening, not a vulnerability and not a finding from any security scan — carries a SEC-22 id purely to keep the SEC-17 lineage traceable in this ledger. Raised at PR review for Story 10.51 (SEC-17, https://trello.com/c/ki430erK, PR https://github.com/rcchirwa/shopify-mcp/pull/120) on 2026-07-28: SEC-17 made `register_webhook` fail closed by default and added `WEBHOOK_ALLOW_ANY_HOST=true` as an explicit opt-out restoring the old warn-and-proceed behavior, but nothing surfaced that the opt-out was active — a deployment could carry it indefinitely with no visible signal outside `.env`. Trello: https://trello.com/c/6a68944ca3c5a30d16dd6dc6 (Story 10.56, Epic 10).

### Closed

| # | Item | How it closed |
|---|------|----------------|
| SEC-22 | ~~`create_server()` (`src/shopify_mcp/server.py`) constructed `Settings()` inline for `configure_logging()` and discarded it; `webhook_allow_any_host` was never read on the startup path, so `WEBHOOK_ALLOW_ANY_HOST=true` had no observable effect until someone actually called `register_webhook`~~ | New leaf module `src/shopify_mcp/startup_warnings.py::startup_warnings(settings) -> list[str]`, kept separate from `server.py` (which stays in the coverage `omit` list — unchanged, and `tests/architecture/test_src_layout.py` still passes unmodified) so the check itself is fully unit-testable. `create_server()` now captures its `Settings()` instance in a local, reuses it for `configure_logging()` and `ShopifyClient(settings)` instead of constructing it twice more, and logs each `startup_warnings(settings)` message at WARNING after `configure_logging()` has attached the stderr handler. **Startup-only, by design** (recorded in the module docstring): fires once at process start, not on every `register_webhook` call — the point is to catch the *configuration* sitting in a weakened posture, not to duplicate the per-call "EXTERNAL DOMAIN" annotation `tools/webhooks.py` already shows in the tool's own preview output. The warning text names the consequence (`register_webhook` accepts any https host, `WEBHOOK_ALLOWLIST_HOSTS` is bypassed) and how to restore the default posture, not just the flag name. Verified it survives the SEC-19 logger clamp (Story 10.53): the clamp raises `gql`/`urllib3`/`requests` to WARNING but leaves `shopify_mcp.*` alone, and the warning is logged through `shopify_mcp.server`, a first-party logger. Tests assert on captured stderr handler output (the pattern Story 10.53 established), not just on the returned list. `.env.example` and `README.md` now note that enabling the opt-out produces a startup warning. |

## 2026-08-11 — Story 10.57 (SEC-23 — stale transitive `cryptography` pin, dependency-audit gate repair)

Trello: https://trello.com/c/yun1Dq4O (Story 10.57, Epic 10). **Not an exploitable hole in this codebase** — recorded here as a CI-gate repair and lockfile-hygiene fix, not a vulnerability closure in the usual sense.

### Closed

| # | Item | How it closed |
|---|------|----------------|
| SEC-23 | ~~`pip-audit -r requirements.lock --strict` and `pip-audit -r requirements-dev.lock --strict` both flagged `cryptography==49.0.0` for PYSEC-2026-3552 (a PKCS#7 decryption-oracle in `pkcs7_decrypt_der`/`_pem`/`_smime`), failing the `dependency-audit` job on every PR regardless of what it changed. `cryptography` is transitive and undeclared — pulled in only via `mcp` → `pyjwt[crypto]` for JWT signature verification — and neither lockfile had been re-resolved since Story 10.40 first generated them, so the pin simply aged into the CVE window. `grep -rn "import cryptography\|from cryptography\|pkcs7\|PKCS7\|import jwt\|EnvelopedData" src/ tests/` confirmed no first-party code imports `cryptography`, PKCS#7, or JWT — the vulnerable decrypt path is unreachable here.~~ | Regenerated both lockfiles pinning `cryptography==50.0.0` (the current release; nothing caps it). **Approach used: approach 1 (regeneration, no `pyproject.toml` edit), with the `--upgrade-package cryptography` flag needed to make it take effect** — a bare re-run of the documented command left the pin at 49.0.0 unchanged, because pip-tools treats an existing output file as a pin hint and won't move an unconstrained package unless told to. This is exactly the gap in README's old playbook: "bump the floor in `pyproject.toml`" only applies to a *declared* dependency, and `cryptography` isn't one. `--upgrade-package` re-resolved only `cryptography` (and nothing it forces) — diffed both lockfiles against pre-regen copies and confirmed zero other packages moved. `requirements-audit.lock` needed no change (`grep -c cryptography requirements-audit.lock` → 0; it pins `pip-audit`'s own deps, not this project's). Verified in a fresh Python 3.11 venv built from the regenerated `requirements-dev.lock` with `--require-hashes`: install succeeds, `tests/architecture/test_lockfiles.py` passes unmodified (9/9), both `pip-audit --strict` runs now exit 0 with zero known vulnerabilities, and the full offline suite passes at 100% coverage with no test breakage from the bump. README.md's "Regenerating the lockfile" section now documents the transitive-dependency case and the `--upgrade-package` workaround so the next transitive CVE doesn't re-derive this. The `dependency-audit` job firing here is the gate working as designed, not a new finding — the fix restores it to green, it doesn't patch a live vulnerability in this server. |

CI clean: `shopify-mcp-check-deps` + ruff + `ruff format --check` + mypy + 1347 offline tests at 100% coverage + both `pip-audit --strict` runs clean.

## 2026-08-11 — Story 10.49 (SEC-16 — live webhook runner exposed order PII to httpbin.org)

Source: triple-threat security pass during Story 10.45 (2026-07-25). Trello: https://trello.com/c/Zatbkoou (Story 10.49, Epic 10). `tests/live/test_webhooks.py` registered a real `ORDERS_CREATE` webhook against the configured live store, pointed at `https://httpbin.org/post` — a public third-party request bin. Between registration and cleanup, any order placed on that store would POST its full payload (customer name, email, shipping address, line items) to a host nobody involved controls. Cleanup was already guaranteed (the `finally` block deleted the subscription even on failure); the exposure was the window, not a leaked resource, and nothing gated the runner from executing against a production store.

### Closed

| # | Item | How it closed |
|---|------|----------------|
| SEC-16 | ~~`tests/live/test_webhooks.py` registered a real `ORDERS_CREATE` webhook pointed at `https://httpbin.org/post`; nothing gated the runner from executing against whatever store `SHOPIFY_STORE_URL` pointed at, production included~~ | Three guards now run before any API call: (1) `SHOPIFY_MCP_ALLOW_LIVE_WEBHOOK_TEST=1` must be set explicitly or the runner exits before touching the API; (2) the configured store must report `shop.plan.partnerDevelopment: true` or the runner refuses to register against it; (3) the registration endpoint is read from `WEBHOOK_RECEIVER_URL` — a project-controlled host, never a third party — rather than a hardcoded third-party URL. `register_webhook` itself (SEC-17 / Story 10.51) additionally requires that host to be listed in `WEBHOOK_ALLOWLIST_HOSTS` before it will register, unless the operator has set the `WEBHOOK_ALLOW_ANY_HOST=true` escape hatch, in which case that extra check is bypassed and any https host is accepted — the guarantee this story adds is "never httpbin.org," not "always allowlist-checked," which depends on that pre-existing, independently configurable setting. The test topic was also swapped from `ORDERS_CREATE` to `PRODUCTS_UPDATE`, which exercises the identical register/list/delete round trip without any customer PII ever entering a delivered payload, further shrinking the registration-window exposure. **Rejected alternatives:** a pure localhost receiver (approach 1's localhost-sink variant) was not implemented — Shopify's `webhookSubscriptionCreate` does not synchronously probe endpoint reachability at registration time (it validates scheme/format only and delivers asynchronously with retries), so a localhost URL would register but could never receive a delivery from Shopify's servers, making it useless for the end-to-end verification this runner exists to provide. Reusing the already-allowlisted `WEBHOOK_RECEIVER_URL` avoided standing up new infrastructure while still fully removing the third party. **Residual verification gap:** `shop.plan.partnerDevelopment` is the documented Shopify Admin API field for partner-created development stores; this was not empirically verified against a live store in this change (no live credentials available in the implementing environment) — verify it reads `true` against your actual dev store before relying on it in a real run, per the manual dev-store verification this story's AC requires. |

CI clean: ruff + `ruff format --check` + mypy + full offline suite at 100% coverage. `tests/live` remains excluded from default discovery — this change adds no credential-dependent runner to CI; the fix can only be verified by hand against a real development store (see PR body for that run's output).

## 2026-07-28 — Story 10.52 (SEC-18 — closing-tag neutralization case-sensitivity, delivered via SEC-21)

Trello: https://trello.com/c/AyZG3fsy (Story 10.52, Epic 10). Recorded here for traceability: SEC-18 was implemented and reviewed on its own branch, but never merged as a separate change. Story 10.55 (SEC-21) was cut from a `main` that did not yet contain it, so SEC-21's implementation re-derived the case-insensitive/whitespace-tolerant regex and extended it. Merging 10.52 afterwards would have *regressed* `wrap()` to the narrower pattern, so the branch was retired and SEC-18 is closed by SEC-21's commit instead. No SEC-18 behaviour is missing from `main`.

### Closed

| # | Item | How it closed |
|---|------|----------------|
| SEC-18 | ~~`wrap()`'s only escape defence was `str.replace(_CLOSE_TAG, _CLOSE_TAG_NEUTRALIZED)` — case-sensitive against the exact literal `</UNTRUSTED-DATA>`, so the same tag in any other casing (`</untrusted-data>`, `</UnTrUsTeD-DaTa>`) or with interior whitespace passed through verbatim, forged the closing delimiter, and let third-party store content escape the data region into instruction context~~ | Closed by SEC-21 (Story 10.55, https://trello.com/c/DGE7v3oj), whose compiled `_CLOSE_TAG_PATTERN` is case-insensitive and whitespace-tolerant — a strict superset of what SEC-18 implemented — and additionally covers NFKC-foldable confusables, the underscore separator, and Unicode dash variants. See the SEC-21 entry above for the full description and residual gaps. |

## 2026-07-28 — Story 10.55 (SEC-21 — Unicode-confusable and separator-variant closing-tag neutralization)

Source: follow-up to SEC-18 (Story 10.52, https://trello.com/c/AyZG3fsy), which hardened `wrap()`'s closing-tag neutralization to be case-insensitive and whitespace-tolerant via a compiled regex, but explicitly deferred two residual gaps: Unicode homoglyph/confusable characters and the underscore separator variant (`UNTRUSTED_DATA`). Trello: https://trello.com/c/DGE7v3oj (Story 10.55, Epic 10).

### Closed

| # | Item | How it closed |
|---|------|----------------|
| SEC-21 | ~~`wrap()`'s closing-tag neutralization (post-SEC-18) still missed the underscore separator variant (`</UNTRUSTED_DATA>`) and Unicode-confusable spellings — fullwidth bracket/slash characters (U+FF1C, U+FF0F, U+FF1E) and Unicode dash characters (hyphen U+2010, non-breaking hyphen U+2011, en-dash U+2013) standing in for `<`, `/`, `>`, and `-`~~ | `wrap()` now NFKC-normalizes `text` before scanning it. Verified empirically (not assumed) that NFKC folds the fullwidth bracket/slash confusables to ASCII 1:1, but does **not** fold the dash confusables to ASCII `-` (non-breaking hyphen only folds as far as plain hyphen U+2010; en-dash is untouched) — so those codepoints are matched explicitly in the closing-tag regex's separator character class alongside `-` and `_`. Because every fold relevant to this pattern is 1-codepoint-in/1-codepoint-out, the wrapper operates entirely on the normalized copy for the rest of the call (no positional misalignment risk from unrelated NFKC-driven length changes elsewhere in the value). The single compiled `_CLOSE_TAG_PATTERN` (case-insensitive, whitespace-tolerant around `/` and the separator, accepting `-`/`_`/the Unicode dash confusables) subsumes SEC-18's case/whitespace fix, since main had not yet merged SEC-18 when this branch was cut. Payload-preservation contract unchanged: neutralized, not dropped. `/gss-dual-review` caught a real gap the initial dash list missed — figure dash (U+2012), em-dash (U+2014), horizontal bar (U+2015), and minus sign (U+2212) also don't fold to ASCII `-` under NFKC and were slipping through unneutralized (empirically confirmed); added to the character class and covered by new tests. The reviewer also flagged zero-width/invisible characters (ZWSP U+200B, ZWNJ/ZWJ U+200C/U+200D, BOM U+FEFF) inserted inside the delimiter as a second, confirmed bypass — deliberately left open rather than fixed by blanket-stripping, since ZWJ/ZWNJ have legitimate uses in emoji sequences and Persian/Indic script rendering and indiscriminate stripping would corrupt legitimate shopper content; documented as a residual gap in the module docstring for a future story to solve properly. **[Annotated 2026-08-18: that residual is now closed by Story 10.70 (SEC-21-zerowidth) — see the entry at the top of this file. The "solve properly" this entry asked for was to widen *detection* rather than strip, which neutralizes a zero-width-laden closer while leaving legitimate emoji-ZWJ and Persian/Indic content byte-for-byte intact, so this entry's stated objection is satisfied rather than overridden. The module docstring's "Known residual gap" paragraph has been replaced accordingly.]** **[Annotated 2026-09-05 by Story 10.71 (SEC-21-confusables): this entry's per-position reasoning was sound for the dashes and incomplete for the brackets and slash. Having verified that the *fullwidth* bracket/slash forms fold to ASCII, it concluded those positions needed no explicit class — but never enumerated the bracket/slash confusables NFKC also leaves alone (U+2215, U+2044, U+29F8, U+2039/U+203A, U+3008/U+3009, U+276E/U+276F), and never considered homoglyph letters inside `UNTRUSTED`/`DATA` at all. All eight were confirmed passing through un-neutralized in 2026-08 and are now closed; see the SEC-21-confusables entry at the top of this file.]** Module docstring rewritten to describe the full defense with no stale "known gap" language for anything now covered. Full gate chain green: ruff + `ruff format --check` + mypy + 1321 offline tests at 100% coverage. |

## 2026-07-28 — Story 10.54 (SEC-20 — sanitize control characters in the retry log label)

Trello: https://trello.com/c/kDhUrdKw

| ID | Problem | Fix |
|----|---------|-----|
| SEC-20 | ~~`ShopifyClient._with_retry` (`client.py:230`) logs `label` through a single-line stderr formatter that never neutralized CR/LF. `fetch_bytes` (`client.py:350`) built its label as `f"fetch {url}"` from a caller-supplied, model-controlled `url` (reachable via indirect prompt injection); a URL containing an embedded newline followed by a plausible timestamped line could forge a second, spoofed-looking log entry. The audit-log path (`tools/_log.py:67`) already sanitized CR/LF, but the escaping had never been applied to this second sink~~ | New shared helper `tools/_scrub.py::sanitize_control_chars(text)` (escapes `\r`→`\\r`, `\n`→`\\n`) — a single control-character-escaping implementation reused by both sinks. `tools/_log.py::log_write` now delegates to it (order preserved: sanitize, then `cap`, so escaped tokens still count toward `MAX_DESC_LEN`). `client.py::fetch_bytes` now builds its retry label as `f"fetch {sanitize_control_chars(url)}"`. `execute`'s label (`f"op={op_name}"`) was deliberately left unsanitized — `op_name` comes from a regex match against an in-code GraphQL query string, never caller-controlled, so it carries no injection risk (documented inline at the call site). Severity was **Low** — log-integrity only, no authorization or data-access decision reads this log. |

## 2026-07-28 — Story 10.53 (SEC-19 — root logger let gql dump request/response bodies)

Trello: https://trello.com/c/lN4WTLaW (Story 10.53, Epic 10). The scan panel raised this as two findings on the same root cause, deduplicated by file/line rather than cause — **one fix closes both**.

### Closed

| # | Item | How it closed |
|---|------|----------------|
| SEC-19 (F2 + F4) | ~~`configure_logging` (`logging_config.py`) set the level (F4, line ~25) and attached the stderr handler (F2, line ~40) on the **root** logger. The pinned `gql==3.5.3` `RequestsHTTPTransport` sets no level of its own and logs `log.info(">>> %s", json.dumps(payload))` / `log.info("<<< %s", response.text)` — inheriting root's effective level, every Shopify Admin API request (full query + all GraphQL variable values) and every response body was written verbatim to stderr at the *default* `log_level` (INFO), not just in a debug mode. Concretely: the plaintext discount code in `create_price_rule_discount_code` (masked as `code=***` in the audit log per SEC-12, but not in the gql trace), and the `stagedUploadsCreate` response body — signed upload URL + signing `parameters` — that `tools/media/_upload.py` deliberately withholds from callers per SEC-11. Both leaks bypassed `client.py`'s variable-key-only logging and the redaction intent documented at `docs/architecture-tech-debt.md:347`, because the leak happens inside the transport library, below the layer that does redaction.~~ | After `root.setLevel(level)` in `configure_logging` (`src/shopify_mcp/logging_config.py`), clamp `gql`, `urllib3`, and `requests` loggers to `max(level, logging.WARNING)`. Holds at WARNING even when `LOG_LEVEL=DEBUG`. Root logger configuration (F2's handler, F4's level) is otherwise unchanged — first-party code all logs under `shopify_mcp.*` and is unaffected. **Opt-in decision: none added.** No caller in this codebase has a use case for raw gql wire tracing; `client.py` already logs variable *keys* (not values) as the supported debugging path. If a genuine need for full payload/response tracing appears later, add a narrowly-scoped `debug_gql_trace` setting documented as secret-bearing in `.env.example`/`README.md`, rather than removing the clamp. Tests: effective-level assertions for all three loggers at default (INFO) and `LOG_LEVEL=DEBUG`; a captured-stderr assertion that an INFO record on `gql.transport.requests` produces no output (the acceptance-critical case — proves the handler never sees it, not just that the logger level would suppress it); confirms `shopify_mcp.*` INFO and `gql` WARNING records still emit. `tests/conftest.py`'s `_reset_root_logger` fixture extended to also reset the three clamped loggers to `NOTSET` between tests. |

CI clean: `shopify-mcp-check-deps` + ruff + `ruff format --check` + mypy + full offline suite at 100% coverage.

## 2026-07-28 — Story 10.51 (SEC-17 — webhook endpoint allowlist fails closed)

Source: security audit. Trello: https://trello.com/c/ki430erK (Story 10.51, Epic 10). `register_webhook`'s only control was `_check_endpoint` (`tools/webhooks.py`), which fail-*opened* when `WEBHOOK_ALLOWLIST_HOSTS` was unset (the shipped default) — it returned `allowed=True` with a cosmetic `⚠ EXTERNAL DOMAIN` warning instead of refusing, and on the `confirm=True` path that warning rendered only *after* `write_gate` had already created the webhook. There was also no scheme check at all: nothing but Shopify-side validation kept endpoints on `https`.

### Closed

| # | Item | How it closed |
|---|------|----------------|
| SEC-17 | ~~`_check_endpoint` returned `(True, _EXTERNAL_DOMAIN_WARNING)` when `WEBHOOK_ALLOWLIST_HOSTS` was unset instead of refusing, so `register_webhook` fail-opened by default; no scheme check existed at all; and allowlist hostname comparison was a plain `.strip().lower()`, not IDNA-normalized~~ | `_check_endpoint` now rejects any non-`https` scheme before any hostname work. With `WEBHOOK_ALLOWLIST_HOSTS` unset, it returns `(False, …)` naming both `WEBHOOK_ALLOWLIST_HOSTS` and the new `WEBHOOK_ALLOW_ANY_HOST` env var, and performs zero mutation calls at either `confirm=False` or `confirm=True`. `WEBHOOK_ALLOW_ANY_HOST=true` (new `Settings` field, default `False`) is the explicit, documented opt-out that restores the previous warn-and-proceed behaviour byte-for-byte for deployments that intentionally want it. Both the allowlist entries (`Settings.webhook_allowlist_set`) and the request hostname are now normalized through IDNA (`hostname.encode("idna")`, wrapped in `try`/`except UnicodeError` — a failing encode is treated as "not in the allowlist" rather than raising) before comparison, closing a homograph/Unicode-equivalence gap. `.env.example` and `README.md` now present `WEBHOOK_ALLOWLIST_HOSTS` as required for `register_webhook` rather than optional, and document the `WEBHOOK_ALLOW_ANY_HOST` escape hatch. **Judgment call:** the card offered three possible mitigation shapes for the fail-open default; this implementation chose "fail closed with an explicit opt-out" over the other two (hard-refuse-with-no-escape-hatch, or scheme-check-only) so existing deployments aren't stranded — flagged for human confirmation at PR review. |

CI clean: ruff + `ruff format --check` + mypy + full offline suite at 100% coverage.

## 2026-07-18 — Story 10.42 (SEC-09, SEC-10 — consistent batch-size caps + reserved-namespace case-fold)

Source: security audit 2026-07-04. Trello: https://trello.com/c/yKELeIKb (Story 10.42, Epic 10). Metafield tools already capped input lists at 25 (`METAFIELDS_SET_MAX` / `METAFIELDS_DELETE_MAX`), but four list-accepting write tools had no per-call count cap, and the `app--` reserved-namespace guard was case-sensitive (so `APP--` slipped it client-side, even though Shopify rejects it either way).

### Closed

| # | Item | How it closed |
|---|------|----------------|
| SEC-09 | ~~`delete_product_media` (`media_ids`), `reorder_product_media` (`moves`), `update_variant_image_binding` (`variant_media` + nested `mediaIds`), and `update_product_pricing` (`variants`) accepted unbounded list input with no client-side cap~~ | Added named constants — `MEDIA_IDS_MAX` / `MOVES_MAX` (`tools/media/_constants.py`, shared by `delete_product_media` and the nested `mediaIds` in `update_variant_image_binding`) and `VARIANT_MEDIA_MAX` / `PRICING_VARIANTS_MAX` (`tools/catalog_hygiene.py`) — all `= 25`, matching the existing metafield caps. Each cap is enforced before any network call, returning the same "exceeds the N-entry per-call cap (got M)" structured error shape metafields already used. For `update_variant_image_binding`, the `variant_media` validation (including its new cap) was moved ahead of `_resolve_product_gid` so an oversized call is rejected before the handle-lookup network call fires, not just before the mutation. |
| SEC-10 | ~~`tools/catalog_hygiene.py:511`'s reserved-namespace guard was `ns_clean.startswith(RESERVED_NAMESPACE_PREFIX)` — case-sensitive, so `APP--`/`App--` slipped the client-side check (Shopify rejects them anyway; this was a client-side UX/defense-in-depth gap, not an exploitable bypass)~~ | Case-folded the check to `ns_clean.lower().startswith(RESERVED_NAMESPACE_PREFIX)`. |

CI clean: ruff + `ruff format --check` + mypy + 1276 offline tests at 100% coverage.

## 2026-07-17 — Story 10.41 (SEC-04 — systematic untrusted-data wrapping across read tools)

Source: read-only security audit 2026-07-04. Trello: https://trello.com/c/jWndWxHU (Story 10.41, Epic 10). Before this story, only `tools/orders.py` wrapped externally-influenced text in `<UNTRUSTED-DATA>` tags — the wrapping was a per-tool decision rather than a shared policy, so other read tools returned stored third-party content to the model as unlabelled trusted text (indirect prompt-injection surface).

### Closed

| # | Item | How it closed |
|---|------|----------------|
| SEC-04 | ~~Untrusted-data wrapping was per-tool (orders only); metafield values and media alt text reached the model unlabelled~~ | Extracted the `<UNTRUSTED-DATA>` literal + injection reminder into one shared helper, `tools/_untrusted.py` (`wrap(text)` + `INJECTION_REMINDER`); re-pointed `tools/orders.py` to it with byte-identical output for all legitimate content. Applied `wrap()` to the agreed field list and prefixed each affected output with `INJECTION_REMINDER`. There is now a single definition of the tag — no duplicated `<UNTRUSTED-DATA>` literals anywhere.
